INCORPORATING COST-OF-LIVING WAGE ADJUSTMENTS INTO BASE RATES A Short History of Cost-of-Living Wage Adjustments 

-ving The practice of adjusting wage rates on the basis of fluctuations in the cost-of-living dates back almost to the beginning of the century. 1/ Although changes in the cost of living often furnished the basis for reopening the wage provisions of union contracts, contract clauses which tied wages to the cost-of-living achieved no widespread popularity until almost fifty years later.

The growth of cost-of-living wage adjustments during World War II was "nipped in the bud" by the adoption of the Little Steel formula which limited general wage increases to 15% of the average straight time earnings on

January 1, 1941. Not even the inclusion in 1948 of an escalator clause in the well publicized agreement between the prominent General Motors Corporation and the United Automobile Workers, C.I.0. gave the movement any substantial impetus.

It took the Korean emergency with its accompanying rapid rise in living costs to increase the number of workers covered by cost-of-living escalator clauses from less than 800,000 in September 1950 to over 32 million by September 1952.

Escalator Clauses & Wage Stabilization Because the post-Korea stabilization agencies were faced with the widespread adoption of cost-of-living escalator clauses before they applied their wage controls they were no longer able to halt cost-of-living wage increases without risking serious interruption of the defense effort. Faced with that reality, the Economic 

Stabilization Administrator first permitted the continuance of cost-of-living escalator clauses which had been in effect on January 25, 1951, the date of the wage freeze. 2/ 

Prevalence of Cost of Living Adjustments, Nelson M. 

Bortz, Monthly Labor Review July 1, 1948, pp. 1-7.

General Wage Regulation No. 8, issued March 1, 1951 by Economic Stabilization Administration. 

844772

Later the Wage Stabilization Board permitted the continuance or adoption of cost-of-living escalator clauses with later effective dates provided they conformed with certain requirements, the most important of which being that adjustments in accordance with changes in the cost-of-living were required both upward and downward. In effect this meant that such adjustments did not become part of the basic wage rates. It was this kind of cost-of-living escalator provision which was adopted throughout the railroad industry.

This "legalization" of cost-of-living escalator clause. while it solved one problem created another, namely the obvious inequity that parties who agreed to an escalator clause were not limited by the 10% wage increase ceiling while others were so limited. This was solved by the Wage Stabilization Board by a further provision that in the absence of an escalator clause, wages could be increased every six months to restore the "loss in the real value of wages and salaries from January 1951 to the date of the increase."3/ "3/ Since these adjustments were not reversible when the cost-of-living declined, they became, in effect, increases in the basic wage rates. 

In preventing the first inequity, the Board created at least the potentiality for another, namely, the situation whereby a decline in the cost-of-living would require a wage reduction for employees who had received their cost-of-living increases in accordance with an escalator clause but would not have a similar consequence for those who had received their cost-of-living wage adjustment in accordance with the alternative provision whereby such increases were immediately incorporated into the basic rates. The Board recognized this inequity and by interpretation of its regulation permitted the parties, when negotiating a new contract, to incorporate cost-of-living adjustments into their base rates thereby establishing a higher floor below which cost-of-living adjustments could not fall.4/ 

3/ Wage Stabilization Board General Wage Regulation No. 8, 

Revised, Sec. 4. 

4/ Wage Stabilization Board, Interpretation 8A-11, 

July 1, 1952. - 2

Incorporating Cost-of-Living Adjustments into the Base Rates 

19 During the period of wage stabilization some companies and unions did incorporate all or part of their cost-of-living increases into the base rates. 

However, when the same General Motors Corporation and the United Auto Workers, C.I.Q., who pioneered the modern use of these provisions, agreed to incorporate practically all of their cost-of-living increase out of 24 cents, leaving 5 cents as a "float" which could fluctuate downward with the cost of living into the base rates, in May 1953, the trend toward such incorporation was really under way. In the General 

Motors situation the union used the occasion, when it was necessary to amend the escalator clause to conform with the recently revised Bureau of Labor Statistics Consumers Price Index, to demand and secure this incorporation of 19 cents an hour into the base rates even though there was some question about their right under the contract to reopen this matter. 

In the railroad industry (specifically in the contract with the Brotherhood of Locomotive Engineers) 

it was also necessary to amend the escalator clause to conform with the new B.L.S. Consumers Price Index. This was done without any attempt to simultaneously negotiate the incorporation of the cost-of-living increases into the base rates during the term of the contract. 

However, in the wake of the General Motors settlement, a large number of companies and unions agreed to incorporate cost-of-living increases into the base rates. This will be seen from the following listing taken from the principal sources of information on wage settlements, The Bureau of Labor Statistics, the Bureau of National Affairs and the National Industrial Conference Board.

It may be seen from this table that some of the agreements, particularly those in the automobile and related industries and those to which the United Automobile Workers, C.I.O. were a párty, followed the pattern established in the General Motors settlement, namely incorporation of most of the cost-of-living increases into the base rates but leaving a small "float", generally 5 cents or less, which was the extent to which later downward adjustments made in accordance with the continuing escalator clause could go. They constituted less than 40% of the total. 

Most of the agreements over 60% called for incorporation of all of the cost-of-living increases into the base rates. Approximately two-thirds of these also continued the escalator clause providing for further increases if the cost-of-living rose but no decreases below the newly established base rates if the cost-ofliving was to fall. One third of these incorporated all of the cost-of-living increases into the base rates and then discontinued the escalator clause completely. This latter practice was followed in the Railroad industry's agreements with three Brotherhoods representing other train and engine service crafts. 

If the agreements of the United Automobile

Workers, C.I.O., which followed the pattern set by the union's top officials in the General Motors negotiations are excluded, the tendency to include all of the past cost-of-living increases in the wage base becomes even more apparent. Of these agreements (1.e. other than those involving the United Automobile Workers, C.I.0.) only one out of five provided for incorporation of less than the full cost-of-living increases into the base rates. Four out of five incorporated all the past costof-living increases into the wage base; half of these continued the escalator clause and half of these did as was done elsewhere in the railroad industry and abandoned the cost-of-living escalator clause. - 8 about 63%

1. Introduction 

Decisions about changes in wage rates for a particular group of employees depend on two different kinds of consideration: (a) Factors which apply equally to all employees of the company or industry. This includes such things as changes in the cost of living, changes in labor productivity, the past and prospective profitability of the company or industry, and the movement of wages in other industries throughout the economy. (b) Considerations relating to the relative wage rates of different occupational groups within the company or industry. A gen- eral wage increase in an industry need not be uniformly applied to all occupational groups. It can be used, and frequently is used, as an opportunity to realign the internal wage structure. 

Moreover, the concept of "uniform treatment" is ambiguous. 

It could conceivably mean either an equal percentage increase or an equal cents per 

hour increase, and these have different effects on the wage structure. An equal cents per hour increase implies that percentage differentials were previously too wide and need to be reduced. An equal percentage increase implies that percentage differentials should be maintained at their existing level. Any general increase, therefore, involves a deliberate or implied judgment about proper wage relationships between various occupational groups. 

This statement is concerned solely with the second issue, the problem of occupational differentials. I hope to show that the issue is sufficiently important to deserve serious consideration by this Board,

I 

The and to suggest some possible guide-lines for such consideration. shall not attempt to resolve the issue or to suggest any specific size of differential between engineers and firemen. This would involve additional study and is properly a matter for the Board to determine. argument is simply that the issue is important, that it deserves more thought than previous Boards have given to it, that any decision in this case will in effect be a decision concerning wage differentials, and that such a decision should be made explicitly and deliberately rather than by indirection.

The statement consists of five main sections: 

2. A discussion of why wage differentials are important in personnel administration and in collective bargaining. 

3. An examination of different possible methods of determining proper occupational differentials. 

4. A discussion of absolute (cents 

per hour) differentials versus percentage differentials. 

5. Some applications of the principles discussed in Sections 

2 - 4 to the circumstances of the present dispute. 

6. General conclusions. 

2. The Importance of Wage Differentials 

That relative wage rates are important scarcely needs to be argued. Workers certainly give this factor heavy weight in deciding whether their wages are reasonable or not. In a study which the writer made in New Haven, Connecticut, several years ago, some eight hundred

workers were asked whether they regarded their wages as fair or unfair, and for what reasons. Of these workers, about one-third based their answer specifically on relative wage rates, i.e., on how their wages compared with those of other workers in the same company, or with workers doing similar work in other companies. Another large group, including almost one-third, based their answers mainly on the kind of work they were doing. They seemed to feel that a particular kind of work was worth just about so much. While wage rates for other kinds of work were not mentioned explicitly, they were clearly in the background of the worker's thinking.

About one-third of the group based their answer on the adequacy or inadequacy of their earnings to cover the living costs of their families. It is interesting, however, that this absolute measure of the adequacy of wage rates was not mentioned as frequently as were various kinds of relative comparison. It is also interesting that quite similar conclusions were reached by Professors Myers and Shultz of M. I. T. in a study of workers in Nashua, New Hampshire. Examination of these and other studies leaves no doubt of the importance which workers attach to relative wage comparisons.1

This basic attitude of employees is reflected in both union and management thinking. The determination of relative wage rates within a company is recognized as a major problem in modern personnel management. Elaborate systems of job evaluation have been established 

1. G. Reynolds and Joseph Shister, Job Horizons, New York, Harper Brothers, 1949, Pp. 20-30. 

Charles Myers and George Shultz, Dynamics of a Labor Market, New York, Prentice-Hall, 1951, Pp. 125-130.

- in some companies to measure job content and determine proper differentials. Virtually all large companies give explicit attention to the problem in one way or another. In the case of unionized companies, occupational differentials are typically a major issue in collective bargaining.

Management, unions, and employees are thus agreed that relative wage rates are important. But just why are differentials important? There are at least four reasons, each of which deserves brief mention. (a) Recruitment 

The basic importance of wage differentials arises from their role in directing an adequate supply of labor into each occupation in the economy. Workers have a choice - a wide choice in their early years, a narrower range of choice later on as to what occupation they will enter. Each occupation stands to some extent in competition with every other. Occupations requiring high skill and long training, in particular, must offer a sufficient premium to induce people to acquire the necessary skill and experience. 

This is not just an academic point. There is evidence that inadequate differentials are a practical problem in certain industries in the United States, and an even more serious problem in some of the European countries. From the mid-'thirties until the late 'forties there was a steady reduction, percentage-wise, in differentials between high-skilled and low-skilled workers in the United States. By the late 'forties, one could frequently find low-skilled manufacturing workers paid on an incentive basis earning more than skilled maintenance workers

paid on an hourly rate. This naturally made it difficult to persuade youngsters that it would be worth their while to invest several years in training for a skilled craft. Companies using apprenticeship or other types of training program often reported a deficiency of trainees. Partly as a result of this problem, there has been considerable effort in recent years to reverse the previous trend and to widen the cents per hour differential between occupational groups. A 

number of important union-management agreements have provided larger hour increases for the top occupational groups than for cents lower 

per 

skilled employees. Many companies not operating under union contract have adopted a flat percentage basis for wage increases in order to achieve the same result.

In some of the European countries the situation is much more acute than it is here. In France, as a result of rapid inflation plus a government policy of giving equal cents per hour increases to 

workers of all grades, the differential between skilled craftsmen and laborers has shrunk to little more than 10 per cent. Vocational schools, apprenticeship programs, and employment service offices throughout the country report spot shortages of craftsmen and, more serious for the future, a great shortage of trainees. There is general recognition in government circles that differentials have been unduly reduced and should be widened as rapidly as financial circumstances permit.

England appears to be in a position intermediate between Differentials have not shrunk so dras- 

France and the United States. tically as in France; but they have shrunk appreciably and there are serious shortages of craftsmen and trainees. Here, too, efforts are

being made to correct the situation through equal percentage increases or through graduated cents 

per hour increases which give more to the upper occupational groups. (b) Promotion

In many cases workers for the more highly-skilled jobs in a company are secured by promotion from lower jobs within the company rather than by outside recruitment. Most management people seem to regard this as good practice. Under union contracts, promotion from within is frequently stipulated by job-bidding or straight seniority clauses. 

It is

These clauses give certain workers first chance at vacant jobs, but usually do not require a worker to accept a vacancy. still up to management to make it worth the man's while. For this reason the higher jobs on a promotion-ladder normally carry a wage premium over the lower jobs. Promotion brings an increase in earnings along with an increase in job duties. Unless the wage increase is sufficient, workers may refuse to shift from their present job and vacancies will go unfilled. A proper system of wage differentials, in short, is just as important for internal recruitment as it is for outside recruitment. 

(c) Morale and Productivity 

Inability to attract a sufficient number of workers is only one possible consequence of inadequate differentials. There may be other consequences having to do with workers' performance on the job.

It was pointed out earlier that workers attach much importance to relative wage rates. Within their own company or production unit they have definite ideas about the relative ranking of different jobs, which jobs ought to pay more than others, and how much more. If the existing wage structure departs too far from the employees' own ranking of different jobs, some will regard themselves as seriously underpaid. This is bound to affect their attitude toward the employer and their performance on the job. Wherever they have a choice between doing a little more and a little less, they may choose to do a little less. Acute dissatisfaction among even a minority of employees is likely to spread through and affect the whole organization. These effects are difficult to trace and to demonstrate statistically. Anyone with industrial experience will testify, however, that workers' satisfaction or dissatisfaction with their jobs and with the treatment given them by the employer does have a direct impact on their job performance. (d) Equity Considerations

There are some cases, perhaps a good number of cases, which are not covered very effectively by any of the above considerations. 

Take, for example, the case of the locomotive engineers. They are obliged to accept promotion from the fireman's roster as vacancies occur, and the option which most workers have of accepting or refusing a promotion is denied them. The average age of engineers is so high (58.5 years at the present time) that they have practically no opportunity of shifting to other occupations. Indeed, they cannot even shift to another employer within the railroad industry because of the

loss of seniority involved. The extent to which they can express dissatisfaction with their wage rates through job performance is limited in various ways. They are held to a certain standard of performance by a network of union, company, and government regulations, as well as by their own sense of professional responsibility. 

There are probably other occupations which present this same combination of circumstances. There are cases, in short, in which a group of people are "stuck" with their occupation for better or for worse, and have no effective way of hitting back against a worsening of their relative position. Does this mean that in these cases wage differentials are of no consequence and can be narrowed as much as we may please? I do not think so. On the contrary, it is particularly necessary in such cases to consider whether a worsening of the group's relative position is equitable in the light of all the circumstances. If an injustice is being done, the fact that the people concerned have no effective way of retaliating is not an excuse for the injustice. 

3. Methods of Determining Occupational Wage Differentials 

Granted that wage differentials are important, and that one wants to establish proper differentials in a particular situation, how does one go about it? There are a number of different approaches to determination of occupational differentials, each of which deserves brief discussion, and several of which will usually be found operating in any concrete situation. These are: reliance on the competitive forces of the labor market; systematic analysis of job content; collective

bargaining; custom, status, and other social considerations. a. The labor market approach

The notion that determination of occupational differentials can simply be left to free competion in the labor market has a venerable history in economic theory. The general lines of this approach were developed by Adam Smith, and succeeding writers have not added very much to his analysis. 

The central idea is that, if workers are able to make a free and informed choice among occupations, the wage level of a particular occupation will have to be sufficient to tempt into that occupation as many people as employers want to hire. An occupation which requires years of training will have to pay more than one which requires no training, in order to induce people to invest time and effort in training for it. An occupation which is especially dangerous, or unhealthful, or otherwise unpleasant will have to pay a premium on that account, and so on. In a competitive system, in other words, wage differentials play an equalizing role. Their function is to equalize the net advantages of various occupations in the eyes of the worker who is considering which one to choose. 

There is no doubt that competitive forces are operative in our economy and do contribute to the shaping of wage differentials. 

Jobs which are highly skilled or hazardous or difficult do tend to carry a premium over more routine jobs. If a particular kind of labor is in short supply, its wage rate will tend to rise faster than other wage rates.

This on the one hand induces more people to enter or stay

in the occupation, while at the same time giving employers an incentive to reduce their use of this scarce labor. The spectacular increase in wages of farm laborers and domestic servants since 1940 is a case in point.

At the same time, few would claim that competition is capable af making very fine or very accurate adjustments in the detailed structure of job rates. The labor market is a notably imperfect instrument, operating effectively only during periods of peak employment, and even then not with the speed and precision one would wish. Many jobs those filled by promotion from within the company never come into the outside labor market at all. Many others are so specialized, so limited to one or a few companies, that there cannot be said to be a general market for them. 

For these and other reasons, the market has been supplemented increasingly by systems of deliberate wage administration. Wages, like most other kinds of price, are influenced but not fully determined by market forces. They are determined by administration and bargaining within outside limits set by the market. 

b. Analysis of job content 

A system of wage administration, whether controlled unilaterally by the employer or bilaterally through collective bargaining, typically involves a detailed schedule of rates to be paid for each job in the company.

The ranking of jobs one above the other in the rate schedule implies a judgment that certain jobs are worth more than others not because of the individuals who happen to be performing 

1

them at a particular time, but because of characteristics inherent in the jobs themselves. The maxim that one should "rate the job, not the man" is a central principle of orderly wage administration. 

The considerations which are generally regarded as important in judging the worth of a job include: (1) educational requirements; (2) length of training and experience required to perform the job satisfactorily; (3) extent of responsibility for such things as the safety and proper maintenance of equipment, safety of fellow-workers, and quality of product; (4) extent of supervisory authority, if any; (5) degree of physical effort and fatigue; (6) degree of mental effort and concentration; (7) extent of physical risks connected with the job; (8) other unpleasant or objectionable features of the job. This is not intended to be a complete listing, but simply to illustrate the kinds of thing which personnel managers typically take into account. 

How far a particular job possesses these or any other specific characteristics is bound always to be a matter of informed judgment. But any employer who sets up a schedule of job rates, or any employer and unions who agree on a schedule of job rates, have in effect expressed an opinion on the relative worth of the jobs in question. All wage administration rests on judgments, open or implied, formal or informal, about job content. 

C. Collective bargaining

Collective bargaining is not so much a separate method of wage administration as a device by which representatives of the employees can participate in and exert influence on whatever system the

employer happens to be using.

The pure collective bargaining approach to wage administration and wage differentials can be stated about as follows: the function of the union is to canalize and make effective the sentiments of its members concerning proper wage relationships in the plant. If a group of members feel that their job is underrated relative to others, and if the officers consider the case a strong one, it is the union's duty to press for an adjustment as promptly as possible. The union does not want anything to stand in the way of maximum freedom to negotiate on each and every rate in the plant. A wage rate or wage differential is not "right" unless it has been agreed on by union and management and is satisfactory to the workers concerned. 

By the same token, a wage which has been agreed on in collective bargaining needs no other sanction. It is a correct rate, and does not need the blessing of either the competitive market or the industrial engineer. 

An important consequence of collective bargaining has been to promote standardization of wage differentials throughout a particular industry. Each company acting by itself is likely to come out with considerable variation in the relative ranking of different jobs. 

Job A may be rated above Job B by some companies, but below it by others. The union tries to iron out these differences and arrive at a more or less uniform ranking in all companies. Some of the older and stronger unions have established standard rate schedules which make occupational differentials in different companies of the industry practically identical. Even where this has not been accomplished, the

variation in differentials has usually been considerably reduced. d. Custom and status Over Whether wages are administered by the employer alone or jointly through collective bargaining, considerable weight must be given to past practice and custom in the company or industry. the course of time, workers develop definite opinions about the relative ranking of various jobs. Some jobs are rated as better, higher, more prestigeful than others. Workers then reason that a job which is higher than another should carry a wage premium, and form opinions about the proper size of this premium or differential. 

It is sometimes a little hard to distinguish cause from effect in this matter. A job which has traditionally carried a higher wage rate is likely on that basis alone to be ticketed as a "higher" job. The main point, however, is that the wage rate is a prestige index as well as a source of income. It is a symbol, visible and recognized throughout the work group, indicating the status of the worker and of the job which he performs. 

The ranking of different jobs in terms of prestige and wage level gradually hardens into custom. It is accepted by workers low in the job hierarchy as well as those at the top. Any marked change in wage differentials any change, that is, which wrenches the wage 

structure out of line with the prestige structure which workers have in their minds - is bound to be resented. A change in differentials without adequate justification in terms of job content is certain to be regarded as arbitrary and unfair.

- e. Multiplicity of factors in most situations 

These various approaches to determination of occupational differentials are logically distinct and could be pursued independently. In practice, however, no one approach is likely to be followed to the exclusion of all others. Different considerations are usually balanced against each other in the light of circumstances in a particular case.

Official schedules of job rates, for example, are always tempered by the necessities of the labor market. If the prescribed rate is not high enough to attract the number and quality of workers needed, the rate will be adjusted in one way or another. Rates set through collective bargaining may also come into conflict with market necessities. During the years of high employment since 1940, it has not been uncommon to find bargained rates set lower than the traffic would bear, and to find employers voluntarily paying over the union scale in order to attract labor. 

A practical approach to determination of occupational differentials must be strictly pragmatic, taking into account all relevant factors but not riding any one of them too hard. a. 

4. Absolute and Percentage Differentials 

What constitutes "equal treatment"?

The difference in wage rates for two or more occupations can be expressed in two different ways: in absolute terms (cents-per-hour or dollars-per-year), or in percentage terms. This applies also to changes in relative wage rates over the course of time, and here the two

bases of calculation will yield quite different results. 

Suppose, for example, that in a certain year the rate for Job A was $1.50 per hour, and the rate for Job B was $1.00 per hour. 

Ten years later, one finds Job A at $2.00 and Job B at $1.50. What has happened to "differentials"? On an absolute basis, the differential has remained unchanged at 50 cents per hour. On a percentage basis, however, it has been considerably reduced. The advantage of 

Job A over Job B has fallen from 50 per cent to 33-1/3 per cent. 

Which is the more significant way of looking at the situation? Have "differentials" been reduced or haven't they?

This is far from being an academic quibble. It raises questions of the greatest practical importance. When making a general wage increase covering a variety of occupations, one's first thought may be that the simplest thing is to leave occupational differentials unchanged. But what does it mean to leave differentials unchanged? On an absolute basis of calculation, an equal cents per -hour increase for everyone will have this result. On a percentage basis, however, equal percentage increases will be required.

GORE 

The same issue arises if one approaches the problem in terms of "equal treatment" for all the groups concerned. Are you giving "equal treatment" if you give everyone the same number of cents per hour? Or does "equal treatment" require the same percentage increase for all?

b. 

The importance of percentage calculations in economics It seems reasonably clear that the percentage basis of calculation is the more fundamental, and that it is a necessary startingpoint for practical decisions about wages. This is not to say that one will always come out in the end with a uniform percentage increase for everyone. But unless you have calculated percentage differentials at the outset, and know what a proposed wage change would do to percentage differentials, and are sure that you want this result, you are not in a position to reach a sound decision. 

It is no accident that most of our basic economic calculations are made on a ratio or percentage basis. Price indexes and production indexes are set up on this basis. We speak of prices having risen 10 per cent or 20 per cent as compared with some base period. 

When we try to figure out whether real wages have risen, i.e., whether wage earners now have greater purchasing power than they had at some earlier time, we do so by dividing the percentage increase in consumer prices into the percentage increase in wages. All the standard accounting ratios current assets to current liabilities, labor costs and material costs to value of sales, net earnings to sales or to capital investment, and so on are computed on a percentage basis. 

Job evaluation systems rest on percentage calculations. The rate for 

Job A relative to Job B rests on the percentage which the point score for Job A is of the score for Job B. There is no need to multiply examples. It is clear that to a large extent economic calculation is percentage calculation.

One important reason for this emphasis on percentages is that, in a world of fluctuating price levels and money values, only equal percentage changes in all prices and wages will leave everyone in the economy in the same relative position as before. This is, in fact, the way in which economists define a "neutral" or "smooth" inflation or deflation. Suppose that the general price level, and the total money value of national output (gross national product), have risen by 20 per cent over a short period. This is a neutral inflation only if every price, every wage rate, and the money incomes of every individual and group in the economy have risen by the same 20 per cent. 

In this case everyone would be just where he was before, and the fact that all values had been marked up by 20 per cent would be of no particular importance. 

If, on the other hand, the wage level of group A has risen by 30 per cent, and that of group B by only 10 per cent, it is quite clear that the situation has been altered. Members of group A are better off than before, while members of group B are worse off. The inflation has not been impartial in its effect. Different groups have not had "equal treatment." Equal treatment implies a uniform percentage rate of increase for all. 

C. Some applications to wages

Let us apply this line of reasoning more specifically to the case of wages. 

Suppose that over a period of years, the consumer price index has risen by 100 per cent. (This happens to be very nearly the actual increase over the period 1940-53.) This means that the cost of

living for most wage-earning households will have risen by something like 100 per cent.

There will be a certain amount of variation around the average, particularly as between households whose incomes differ greatly. Different income-levels have different patterns of consumption and, since the prices of all consumer goods never advance at the same rate, the percentage increase in living costs may be greater for some income-brackets than for others. As between workers earning $2,000 a year and workers earning $8,000 a year, this would be a serious qualification. As between workers reasonably close together in income-level, however, it is not a serious qualification and one is justified in supposing that their living costs will have advanced at about the same rate.

Now suppose that, while living costs have been rising by 100 per cent, the general level of wage rates has risen by 150 per cent. (This again is realistic; wage rates typically move up faster than prices in a progressive economy with a rising level of productivity.) 

In this situation, what is required to leave each occupational group in the same relative position as before? Clearly this will require a wage increase of about 150 per cent for each and every occupational group in the system. Some adjustment may be required to take account of the previously-noted fact that the cost of living increase will not be uniform for all income-brackets; but as between workers in neighboring brackets roads 

say the different grades of engine service on the railthis will make only a minor difference.

With this qualification, one can say that only equal percentage increases in all wage rates will enable the standard of living of all workers to rise at the same rate. Only equal percentage increases will enable each occupational group to enjoy the same relative level of living, compared with other groups, after the inflation as before the inflation. If one judges that the relative living standards of different groups were roughly correct at some base date, only equal percentage increases will keep them properly aligned with each other over the course of time. "Equal treatment," from a standard of viewpoint, implies an equal percentage rate of increase in wages. 

This appears quite clearly if one looks at some alternative 

living possibilities. Suppose that, while living costs have risen 100 per cent, the wages of occupation A go up by 200 per cent, those of B by 150 per cent, those of C by 100 per cent, and those of D by 75 per cent. 

Then the impact of the inflation has clearly been very unequal. Members of group A are now able to live roughly twice as well as before; group B has benefited substantially, though not so much as A; group C has barely held its own and its members have made no advance in living standards; the living standards of group D have been materially reduced.

This is the kind of result which is apt to follow from a hour increases to different occupapolicy of giving equal cents 

per 

tional groups during an inflationary period. A typical illustration of what happens under such a policy is shown in Table 1. Everyone has received the same absolute increase of $1.00 per hour. For the highest

job classification, however, this is an increase of 50 per cent, while for the lowest job it is an increase of 100 per cent. The purchasing power of the lower groups have been pushed up much faster than those of the higher groups, and the levels of living of the different occupational groups have been brought much closer together. There has been a telescoping of the wage structure. This shows up clearly in the much smaller percentage wage differentials at the end of the period than at the beginning.

d. The policy issue 

I am not arguing that a reduction of percentage differentials, which means a leveling of living standards, is necessarily or always a bad thing. This is a judgment which must be made according to the circumstances of each particular case. I am arguing that the question of whether a particular reduction of differentials is equitable needs to be raised and answered explicitly. The question should not be begged by ignoring it.

There has been a tendency over the past twenty years to rehour increases as somehow natural and right, gard equal cents 

per 

and simply to ignore the continued shrinkage of percentage differentials. 

This is perhaps the easiest and cheapest policy to pursue during a period of general inflation. There is nothing natural or inevitable about such a policy, however, and there is no reason why it cannot be reconsidered and reversed as circumstances warrant. 

The question of equity, in particular, needs to be squarely faced. To argue that a reduction of percentage differentials is a good thing means, in effect, to argue that differentials were too wide in the first instance - that the top occupational groups were living too well compared with the lower groups, and that a leveling of living standards is socially desirable. This is a perfectly legitimate line of argument, but it should be put forward explicitly and supported by

evidence. Unless adequate evidence is forthcoming, there should be a presumption in favor of maintaining previous differentials intact. a. 

5. Some Applications to the Present Case 

Trends in the fireman-engineer differentials It is necessary now to bring these general principles to bear on the facts of the present dispute. There is no need to repeat the facts about changes in the wages and earnings of firemen and enginemen since 1926 which have been set forth in other statements before this Board. Speaking broadly, there was no drastic change in differentials between the two groups over the years 1926 - 1936. For most classes of service, the percentage differential in average gross hourly earnings was slightly smaller in 1936 than in 1926. The differential in average annual earnings, however, was on the whole somewhat larger in 1936, i.e., more favorable to the engines, Beginning in 1937, however, there was a continuous series of across-the-board dollars and cents wage increases. 

This naturally had the effect of reducing the percentage differential between firemen and enginemen, the reduction being particularly rapid in the years from 1940 to date. This stands out very clearly from the wage statistics, whether one looks at hourly earnings or at annual earnings, and has occurred in every class of service. In the case of average gross hourly earnings, the differential in road passenger service has fallen from 23.6 per cent (1936) to 6.4 per cent (1953); in local and way freight, from 32.4 per cent (1936) to 14.6 per cent (1953); in through

freight from 31.2 per cent (1936) to 13.6 per cent (1953); and in yard service from 28.2 per cent (1936) to 12.6 per cent (1953). There is no clear evidence that the desirability of this continued reduction of percentage differentials has been weighed and decided explicitly by the various emergency boards which have handled wage disputes in the industry. The custom of making uniform cents per hour increases across the board for all occupational groups was widely adopted in American industry during the 1930's, partly because of the pressure of new unions with a high proportion of low-skilled workers in their membership, partly because the political philosophy of the federal administration emphasized leveling up the incomes of the poorest groups in the economy. It was continued during World War 

II, partly for these same reasons, partly because cents per hour increases were likely to be smaller and thus less inflationary than percentage increases. The sharp reductions in percentage differentials to which this policy led were either ignored or brushed aside. 

During the post-war years, however, the policy of equal cents per hour increases has been questioned in many industries and reversed in a number of important cases. The settlements in steel, automobiles, and other industries, discussed in another statement to the Board, are evidence of this new tendency. In my discussions with management people, I find many of them seriously concerned at the reduction in the differentials paid for skill and responsibility, and strongly inclined toward a policy of percentage increases which will protect the position of the skilled man.

In a recent study of wage problems, I interviewed top management officials of about sixty manufacturing companies in my home city of New Haven, Connecticut. Among other things, I asked whether in their opinion general wage increases should be made on a uniform percentage basis or on a uniform cents per 

hour basis.

Almost all of those interviewed expressed a preference for the percentage basis. They gave three main reasons for this view: (1) Percentage changes will maintain a fair differential between low-skilled and high-skilled work, and will provide necessary encouragement for men to work up to higher jobs as vacancies occur. (2) Skilled workers are accustomed to a higher standard of living than less skilled workers. In a period of rising prices, the skilled men need a larger cents per hour increase to maintain the same relative 

standard of living. (3) A larger increase for the top men is justified because of their greater contribution to production. 

These statements of opinion were supported by the actual policies of the companies. Among companies not operating under union contract, the great majority were making general wage increases on a percentage rather than a cents hour basis. Among companies per 

operating under union contract, many were still giving equal cents per 

hour increases because of union pressure in this direction. 

Most managements were resisting this pressure, however, and were arguing at each annual negotiation for a changeover to the percentage basis. In a few cases management had achieved a return to percentage increases despite union opposition.

For The time may have arrived when this issue should be faced squarely in the railroad industry, and the proceedings of this Board may provide an appropriate occasion. It is worth emphasizing again that any decision which the Board makes in this case will be, in effect, a decision about the size of percentage differentials. example, a finding that the enginemen should receive the same number of cents per hour as the firemen in the present wage movement would imply a judgment that percentage differentials should be further reduced. A larger award would be necessary to maintain the percentage differential, and a still larger award to increase it. Since a judgment must be made on this point in any case, it is certainly desirable that it be made consciously and deliberately rather than by indirection. 

It seems reasonable to start from 1936 as a bench mark. 

The economy had by this time partially recovered from the extreme depression of the early 30's, and had not yet suffered the dislocations of the war and postwar periods. Wage relationships existing in 1936 can be taken as more or less normal relationships. object to 1936 as a base period, however, it could be pointed out that wage relationships among the operating groups on the railroads were much the same in 1936 as in the mid-'20's, which were certainly as nearly normal a period as we have had since World War I. 

If anyone should

Taking 1936 - or, if one prefers, 1926 - as a normal period, it would follow that the percentage differential between engineers and firemen in 1954 ought to be about the same as in 1936 unless cause to

the contrary can be shown. The burden of proof, in other words, rests on anyone who argues that the shrinkage in differentials since 1936 has been normal and desirable. A still stronger burden of proof would rest on anyone who proposed to narrow the differential still further as would be the effect, for example, of awarding the engineers a flat 

5 cents per hour in the present case. b. Key issues for consideration by the Board 

Members of the Board should ask themselves, I believe, at least these two questions: (1) Can the reduction of the differentials since 1936 be justified in terms of changes in job duties? Has the engineer's job become less skilled, responsible, strenuous, etc. relative to the fireman's job over the years 1936 - 54? I doubt very much that this question could be answered in the affirmative. (2) Can the reduction in differentials since 1936 be justified on more general grounds of equity and social justice? In order to answer this question in the affirmative, one would have to argue that in 1936 engineers were living better; relative to firemen, than they were entitled to live. Granted this, it would follow that the subsequent leveling of living standards as between the two groups has been socially desirable. Once more, however, it is doubtful whether the basic proposition could be sustained.

The Board might well inquire also whether the reduction in differentials has had an adverse effect on the supply and productive efficiency of the enginemen. For reasons given earlier, however, I would not regard a negative finding on this point as conclusive. 

The 

1

basic question is one of equity. An injustice is not rendered any the less so by the fact that the individuals concerned cannot do, or do not choose to do, anything about it. 

I would underline particularly the odd situation, unique in my experience, which exists in the matter of promotions from firemen to engineer. The normal situation in industry is that a promotion in job title and job duties carries with it a noticeable increase in wages. In this case, however, a man who goes from the top firemen's job to the lowest engineer's job automatically suffers a worsening of his situation. He will either have lower earnings, or will have to work longer hours for the same earnings; and it apparently takes a good many years for him to get back to where he stood as a top fireman. 

A situation in which a man cannot refuse promotion, but in which promotion means a serious loss extending over a number of years, seems certain to produce a deep and abiding sense of injustice in the worker concerned. It is contrary to all rules of sound personnel management. It is a situation which exists, to my knowledge, in no other industry. It did not exist in this aggravated form in the railroad industry until fairly recent years. As far back as 1919, to be sure, there was a small decline involved in the move from top fireman to lowest engineer, but this loss was quickly recovered. The present large decline, and the long period necessary to recover it, are due mainly to wage developments since 1936. 

The situation exists because, while the average level of earnings for firemen and engineers have been brought quite close together, the earnings opportunities on each of the two rosters increase

It would be unwarranted, however, to argue that this Board is precluded by previous settlements from making an independent judgment on the present case. There are circumstances under which uniform treatment of all groups in an industry is virtually compulsory. This is true particularly when one is operating under government controls arising from war or near - war conditions. conclusion of the Erickson Board in 1946 that it was bound by the uniform increases already recommended by two other emergency boards was based directly on the wage stabilization regulations laid down under Executive Order 9697. All the language of that Board's report must be read in this context. Again, the events of 1951 53 were heavily influenced by the control program of the National Wage Stabi lization Board. 

why

Except for the wage movement of 1947 - 48, this is the first time since 1941 that a Board has had an opportunity to review the wage structure of the industry and to reach conclusions independent of government regulation. It seems to me that the opportunity should be taken and put to good effect. To argue that this Board and future boards must never depart from uniform cents per hour increases for 

all trades in the industry is to argue that cents per hour differentials should be frozen for all time to come and that no realignment of the wage structure will ever be possible. This seems clearly untenable. At some point the differential problem will have to be considered. To refuse to do so in the present case would simply push off the problem onto some future board.

whe It is worth noting that the Erickson Board in 1946, although 

it felt bound by stabilization regulations to award a flat cents hour increase, expressed a definite preference for adjustments in per- 

11 of the report centage terms. The relevant passages from Pages 10 are as follows: "After wage increases in terms of percentages have been found justifiable, it has of late become the practice to convert them into cents per hour. This is apparently a device for giving a relatively greater money increase to employees in the lower-wage brackets, and obviously results in a corresponding disadvantage to the higher paid skilled employees who have a higher base rate of pay. If needs and equalization of incomes were the sole or principal considerations in wage adjustments, this conversion of percentage increases into cents per hour would be defensible. The fundamental weakness of such a basis of wage adjustments is, however, that it fails to take into consideration the desirability and validity of preserving well-established relationships between different classes of labor whose differences in basic rates of pay rest on differences in skill and responsibility, risk and experience."

Wage differentials constructed on a foundation of significant variations in skill, responsibility, experience and risk are definitely recognized in the railroad industry and are an important factor in the consciousness and pride of craft. Specifically, an inescapable result of converting percentage wage increases into cents per hour is the narrowing of long-established differentials between

the pay of engineers over firemen and of conductors over brakemen. 

Indeed, prior to 1912, firemen's wages were expressed in terms of a percentage of engineers' wages. We think it is appropriate to observe also that the so-called wage "deductions" of the period 1932 - 35 and the proposed wage reduction of 1938, which were initiated by the carriers, were on a percentage basis. Restoration of said "deductions" was also in terms of percentages. The conversion of a justifiable percentage increase into cents per hour actually diminishes the relative pay increase of engineers and conductors compared with those crafts that have lower standards of basic rates. 

A similarly narrowing effect appears in the wage differentials of other crafts in the railroad industry." "Although the Board recognized the aforementioned disadvantage of cents out 

per hour wage adjustments, it labored under the necessity of conforming to recent and current wage adjustment patterns based on changes in the cost - of siderations of wage stabilization. cents 

living index and con These patterns have been on a per hour basis for all classes of employees within the respective industries, regardless of established differences in job skills. Nevertheless, the Board's considered judgment is that in the future the interests of management and labor will be best served by a return to percentage wage adjustments designed to maintain essential wage differentials resting on differences in such factors as skill, responsibility, and the assumption of risks; but under the circumstances correction of the practice must await future consideration."

Thus a Board operating in 1946 had already concluded that the differential problem was serious and that the time had come for a return to percentage adjustments. Since that time there has been a further sharp reduction of percentage differentials. Surely by 

1954 the time has come for serious attack on a problem which can only grow worse by postponement.

U. A. National medentern Arand 

Arbitration boardine. 172, N.M. B. cave A 4900

BROTHERHOOD EXHIBIT NO. 31 WITNESS: S. H. Unterberger 

St. Louis Missouri 

February 23, 1954 

Mr. Frank Davisson

Assistant Grand Chief Engineer Brotherhood of Locomotive Engineers Congress Hotel 

Chicago, Illinois 

Dear Sir & Brother: 

Complying with your request of February 16, I am inclosing what information I have been able to secure in connection with the promotion rule for firemen on the Terminal 

Railroad Association of St. Louis.

On pages 4 and 5 of the inclosed booklet will be found the promotion rule which was adopted at the time they established compulsory promotion. While the memorandum agreement does not contain any explanation as what brought about this agreement, it is generally known that they were no longer able to get enough firemen to take the examination because of the little difference in pay of engineers and firemen.

I am inclosing also copy of memorandum agreement dated June 26, 1952, wherein they relaxed the promotion rule in order to permit the establishment of the five-day work week. 

It is hoped that will be of some assistance to you. 

If there is anything further I can do to assist, feel free to ask. 

Fraternally yours, /s/ R. H. Wadlow 

Member Division 327

BROTHERHOOD EXHIBIT NO. 3la WITNESS: S. H. Unterberger MEMORANDUM OF AGREEMENT 

все See tr 2993 for text 

Firemen who are eligible for promotion to engineers under the Memorandum Agreement of July 1, 1950 covering progres sive examinations of firemen for promotion to engineers (and who will have completed two years of service on or after September 1, 1952) will be given the opportunity to take their final examinations in the near future.

This temporary abatement of the provisions of the agreement is solely for the purpose of providing the necessary number of engineers required to establish the five day workweek for engineers and firemen on this property effective September 1, 1952. 

Firemen failing to pass the examinations at this time will not be deprived of their rights to promotion under the provisions of the progressive examination agreement. 

TERMINAL RAILROAD ASSOCIATION OF ST. LOUIS By (Signed) John A. Wicks Director of Personnel BROTHERHOOD OF LOCOMOTIVE FIREMEN AND ENGINEMEN By (Signed) G. A. Andrews 

By 

General Chairman 

(Signed) J. A. Mathis

Vice Chairman G. G. C.

BROTHERHOOD EXHIBIT NO. 31b WITNESS: S. H. Unterberger General Committee of Adjustment BROTHERHOOD OF LOCOMOTIVE ENGINEERS Terminal Railroad Association of St. Louis Illinois Terminal Railroad Company Litchfield-Madison Railway Company Manufacturers Railway Company 

September 13, 1953

Mr. Don Beattie, Director Statistical Department 

B. of L. E. 

1124 B. of L. E. Bldg. 

Cleveland 14, Ohio 

Dear Sir & Brother:

As promised, I am inclosing copy of Firemen's seniority roster of the Terminal Railroad Association of St. Louis, dated

May 1, 1950. 

I have placed an X opposite the names of those on this list who have been promoted to engineer, and a check / opposite those who either refused or failed in their examination. of them refused to take the examination. Most The last, or youngest men promoted prior to the adoption of the compulsory promotion rule, July 1, 1950, was J. H. Ringer 

No. 269. of this 269, as I check it, there was 155 who refused or failed promotion, and 114, marked x, that had been promoted. 

A large number of engineers are carried on the firemen's list who are not working as firemen and are working as engineers. 

This is done for convenience in case of reduction in the force and these men are put back firing. 

I hope this may be of assistance to you. 

Fraternally yours /s/ R. H. Wadlow

General Chairman, B. of L. E.

1/ Calculated as follows: 

For yard, road through freight and road local and way freight, 1953 total compensation increased by 5 cents. times straight time hours paid for plus constructive allowance hours paid for plus 7 cents times overtime hours paid for, divided by hours on duty (straight time actually worked plus overtime plus constructive allowance hours).

For passenger, 1953 total compensation increased by 5 cents times total hours, divided by hours on duty. 

2/ Calculated by multiplying, for each class of service, current average hourly earnings (column 1) by 1953 average hours per week. 

3/ Calculated by multiplying, for each class of service, current average hourly earnings (column 3) by % increase over current daily rates required to restore 1919 differential for 300,000 to 350,000 pounds weight on drivers (from Column 8 of Tables 42 through 45). 

4/ Calculated by multiplying, for each class of service, Expected Average Hourly Earnings of 1919 differential reestablished (Column 4) by 1953 average weekly hours. 

5/ Calculated by multiplying, for each class of service, current average hourly earnings (Column 3) by % increase over current daily rates required to restore 1919 differential for 300,000 to 350,000 pounds weight on drivers (from Column 8 of Tables 46 through 49) 6/ Calculated by multiplying Expected Average Hourly Earnings of pre-1937 differential reestablished (Column 6) by

5 Preparatory duties of Engineers on road diesels include the following: 2. 

Inspect exterior of locomotive and running gear for:

Liquids leading from locomotive 

Loose or defective parts

Proper position of mgle cocks and shut off valves observe brake piston travel.

In the engine room with engines running check for: 

Oil, water and fuel leaks 

Check gauges, indicators and switches as follows

Aux. Generator Ammeter 

Starting contactors 

Control air pressure 

Battery Ammeter 

Ground relay 

Main reservoir pressure 

Air compressor intercooler pressure 

Air compressor oil pressure 

Lube oil level 

Engine speed and fuel indicator 

Governor oil level

Water pressure 

Water temperature

Isolation switch 

Lub. oil pressure gauge 

Fuel oil supply gauge

Load regulator 

Lub, oil suction

Water level (Eng. cooling) 

Generator water supply 

Overspeed trip 

Fuel flow 3. 

Check position of transition cut out switches. They should be pinned or sealed in automatic. 4. 

Drain condensation from air brake system. 844782

5. In cab of training "A" unit see that Rotair valve is set in proper Lap position and brake valve is cut out.

THE GENERAL WAGE INCREASES OF RAILROAD ENGINEERS 

Submitted by the

Brotherhood of Locomotive Engineers

THE GENERAL WAGE INCREASES OF RAILROAD ENGINEERS 

The last general wage increase received by railroad engineers was the four cents per hour or 32 cents per day improvement factor adjustment which became effective on December 1, 1952. Since then, as a result of cost of living adjustments, the engineers' wage rates have been below the December 1952 level by 1 cent an hour during the first 3 months of 1953, by 4 cents an hour during the months of April through September 1953 and by 1 cent an hour during the final 3 months of the year. The current level is still 

1 cent an hour below the level at the end of 1952. Although a request for change was made in October 1953, no general wage increase has been negotiated. 

This is in contrast to the developments in most other industries as reported in the Bureau of Labor Statis tics' review of Wage Developments During 1953.1 

"...general wage increases were widespread in most industries during the year. The wage increases which were put into effect in the major manufacturing settlements during 1953 typically ranged from 5 to 9 cents an hour. 

Most existing cost of living escalator clauses were continued and converted to the new Consumer Price Index during 1953. Many of 

Many of these new or revised agreements incorporated a large proportion of previous cost of living increases into base rates, thus reducing the extent to which future price reductions could result in automatic wage rate decreases. A number of major settlements raised 'annual improvement factor' increases due in 1953 and subsequent years erally from 4 to 5 cents an hour.... 

gen- The automobile settlements as well as collective agreements in a number of other industries offset in part previous narrowing of percentage differentials in pay among occupations by giving extra increases to skilled workers.

Most of the 1953 settlements liberalized one or more supplementary wage benefits in addition to increasing wage rates. There was a wide variation in the package of benefit changes. 1/ 

Bureau of Labor Statistics, Monthly Report Current Wage Developments, No. 73, January 1, 1954. 

1 

---

Among the most common changes were increased shift differentials, guarantee of pay for holidays occuring on Saturdays, liberalization of vacation provisions for workers with relatively long service, new or liberalized health and welfare plans and increased pensions. In some industries (notably women's apparel manufacture, trade and transit) the normal work week was reduced with compensating increases in hourly rates."

Corroborating evidence in the size of 1953 general wage increases is which reports as follows: 2/9 the Bureau of National Affairs 

"The overall picture: Approximately 4500 wage settlements were reported during 1953, the largest portion of them during the third quarter. The median increase figure was 8.5 cents (In the first quarter it was 6.3, rose to 8.93 in the second, declined to 8.6 in the third, then down to 8.3 in the fourth.)..." BNA To be sure, there were some groups of workers who received no general wage increase in 1953 or relatively small adjustments (generally 1 or 2 cents up or down) resulting from the operation of cost of living escalator clauses. 

They were concentrated generally in industries affected by a long term downward trend such as coal mining where the product is losing out competitively or northern textiles where the firms are unable to compete with non-union southern establishments.

Not only did the general wage increases of locomotive engineers fail to reach the average established by industry generally in 1953, but comparison with the Bureau of National Affairs' tabulation of general wage increases since 1949 the first year for which these data have been available, indicates that engineers' increases have failed to meet the average in three of those five years. 

Since 1949, the total wage increases of railroad engineers fell behind the total of the average yearly increases in industry generally by 10 cents. This may be seen from the following table: 2

A period that is frequently used for comparing the extent of general wage increases is the period since V-J Day when the wartime wage controls were relaxed and the parties were again permitted to bargain freely over the terms of employment. Data on the wage settlements in this period is provided by the Bureau of Labor Statistics Wage Chronology Series where the Bureau has provided a detailed account of the wage settlements in most of the principal American industries. 

Since V-J Day, railroad engineers have received general wage increases totaling 73 cents an hour or $5.88 per day. Comparison with the total wage increases received in that period in each of the companies and industries covered by the Bureau's Wage Chronologies is shown on the following table.3

Most of these significant companies and industries granted increases which exceeded those granted by the railroad industry to its engineers. In addition, all of them granted some new or additional fringe benefits and most of them granted improvements in all of the four principal types of fringe benefits shown - paid vacations, paid holidays, health and welfare programs and pensions. 

The post V-J Day general wage increases of most of the other industries and companies covered by the wage chronologies exceeded those given railroad engineers by 

5 to 84 cents.

The companies and industries whose post V-J Day general wage increases totaled less than the railroad engineers' were primarily in industries with unusual post-war problems, such as the northern textile industry (cotton, wool and rayon) which has been seriously hurt by low wage non-union southern competition, Western Union which has been in financial difficulty for many years, etc. Even these

el

companies and industries granted in addition to general wage increases, substantial fringe benefits which the railroads did not give. Were the value of the fringe benefits included, the difference from the increase given railroad engineers would be much smaller and in some cases such as 

American Woolen Co., American Viscose Corp. and Glenn L.

Martin Co. would probably equal and might even exceed the railroad engineers' increase.

Finally, as may be seen from the table, many of the companies and industries gave non-uniform adjustments providing larger increases to the higher paid classifications. While it would have been preferable to compare the increases of the railroad engineers, the topmost craft in the railroad industry, with the increases received by the top most craft in these other companies and industry, the data were not available to make this precise comparison. It is clear however, that a proper top craft to top craft comparison would necessarily show a greater discrepancy for most of those companies and industries whose general wage increases exceeded the engineers. All of the companies and industries whose general increases fell short of the engineers except the American Woolen Co. gave their top crafts more than the general increases shown on the table. A top craft to top craft comparison would certainly narrow and might even eliminate the difference with the engineers.

S.P. Train Wreck Fire Kills 3, Injures 83 

TRAIN AFIRE-Flames shoot from windows of Southern Pacific's northbound San Joaquin Daylight Limited after it struck an oil tank and caught fire yesterday. The crash

FRANCISCO 

CHRONICLE 

Feb. 9, 1947 

FLAMING GASOLINE poured over the 

Southern Pacific's Daylight after the red and orange streamliner crashed into a gasoline truck while streaking through the 

San Joaquin valley near Fresno. The truck driver, Philip Mayer of Fresno, who said his vision was impaired by a standing 

THIS WORLD 

-Associated Press freight train, escaped injury but two crewmen and two passengers were killed.

Included in the dead was the engineer, Others escaped because the train stopped quickly. The workable part of the train, four cars untouched by fire, arrived in its Oakland terminal only six hours late. 

S. F. CALL-BULLETIN- Feb. 5, 1947 INTERIOR OF COACH 

Firemen examine burned interior of car of Daylight which was engulfed in flames after explosion of gasoline. -Associated Press Wirephoto. "Mayer contends freight cars parked on a siding obscured his vision. "He says he did not see the approaching Streamliner after he started across the tracks. "But our investigation shows the cars were parked 220 yards from the crossing proper. "This would indicate they did not block the driver's vision."

Times photo occurred at Kingsburg 20 miles south of Fresno. Train's 10 forward cars were enveloped in flames and panic broke out among 450 passengers. Train left Los Angeles at 8:25 a.m.

Truck Driver Charged In Train Wreck Deaths FRESNO, Feb. 6.-District At-1 

torney James M. Thuesen today

LOS ANGELES TIMES 

Feb. 5, 1947 filed a manslaughter complaint Casualties against Phillip Lee Mayer, 21, Fresno truck driver, as the aftermath of the Southern Pacific 

Daylight streamliner wreck in

Kingsburg. 

Four persons lost their lives and 100 passengers were burned and injured in the crackup Tuesday afternoon.

The streamliner was enveloped in flames a few seconds after the engine hit a gasoline tank truck driven by Mayer at the Conejo

Avenue crossing inside the Kingsburg city limits. crack San Francisco

COMPLAINT FILED- 

The felony complaint, charging 

Mayer with four counts of manslaughter, was signed by Captain 

L. M. Eaton of the State Highway Patrol here. Conviction of manslaughter carries a penalty of from one to ten years imprisonment on each count. "I am satisfied from my investigation at the scene of the tragedy and after questioning twenty different persons that 

Mayer did not stop his gasoline laden truck at the crossing," said the prosecutor. "Furthermore, we have witnesses to prove he proceeded over the crossing while the wigwag signal was in operation, and this is criminal negligence."

INQUEST SET- 

Meanwhile, Deputy Coroner 

L. R. Webb has scheduled an inquest into the deaths of the four persons for 2 p. m. tomorrow in the coroner's office here. 

Victims of the crash include: 

George Schneckenberger, 57, Bak-j ersfield, the engineer; Mrs. Sara! 

Badgley, 63, of Dunsmuir; Ernest 

M. Comer, Bakersfield, the fire. man, and Miss Catherine Ann 

Hansen, 18, of Pasadena. 

Thuesen took statements all day yesterday in Kingsburg with a court reporter, assisted by 

Chief Deputy Allan S. Mason. 

Photographs were taken of the. crossing, the overhead wig-wag signal device, and wreckage along the tracks. "We have seven or eight wit nesses who saw parts of the collision, and two of them say the truck did not stop at the crossing," said Thuesen. "These two insist Mayer drove the truck and trailer directly into the path of the approaching streamliner. Seven witnesses have given statements the signal was in operation at the time of the accident."

Streamliner Hits Fuel 

Truck South of Fresno; 

Flames Lash Victims

BY MARVIN MILES, Times Staff Representative KINGSBURG, Feb. 4.-Three persons were killed and 

83 injured late today as fire swept the Southern Pacific's northbound San Joaquin Daylight after it hit a tank truck on the outskirts of this town, 20 miles south of Fresno. 

Exploding with a sound that echoed over a four-mile radius, the liquid fuel wrapped the first few of the train's 

14 cars in flames. 

Then, as this blazing sheath enveloped the 10 forward cars of the crack streamliner, blistering paint and breaking windows in California's fieriest train wreck, panic spread among the 450 passengers.

Joaquin Valley. At the Kings- to thread their way through traffic tangles to reach outlying hosburg Hospital all ambulatory patients were asked to clear their pitals. heds to make room for 23 of the casualties brought in from the wreck. 

Fire Flares Anew Meanwhile the Kingsburg Fire 

Department battled the flames in the train more than two hours before bringing them under control. Once it appeared the fire had been extinguished. 

Then it broke out anew under one coach and again flame swept the car. 

Two dining cars, where seconds before the crash whitecoated waiters were serving passengers a leisurely afternoon meal, became the center of the holocaust as the train, braked at the impact, stopped with those cars beside the flaming trailer. 

Several uniformed servicemen became heroes of the emergency as they turned to rescue work, helping ambulance and emergency Red Cross crews take passengers from the flaming cars and carry them out on stretchers for first-aid treatment. 

The driver of the tank truck, identified as Philip Lee Mayer, 21, of Fresno, escaped unhurt.

The cab and motor section of Among the passengers who described his first impression of the wreck was Machinist's Mate 

3rd Class Philip Walker, re turning to San Francisco to join his ship. "Suddenly we felt a shock," 

Walker said. "Then the entire car was surrounded by flames which kept shooting up outside. 

Everyone got panicky and started shrieking. Several tried to kick out windows and jump." 

Mrs. Robert C. Wheeler of 

North Hollywood, who was on way to her see 

Sacramento, was quoted as saying, "It's awful to those people jumping through windows. I guess Provi dence took care of me." 

The streamliner left the Los 

Angeles Union Passenger Termi. nal at 8:25 a.m. with a baggage car and six coaches destined for

Oakland at the front of the train, a lounge car and two dining cars and four coaches, scheduled for

Sacramento, at the rear of the train. It was due to arrive at San 

Francisco at 8:30 p.m.

Forestall Disaster

HE terrifying railway accident at or explosive material. 

Kingsburg recently, which cost four lives and the injury of a hundred passengers on the Valley Daylight train, emphasizes the need for greater precautions in highway truckage. 

Freight trailers are a highway menace, even when loaded with ordinary material; when the load itself is dangerous, the hazard is multiplied.

LOS ANGELES HERALD 

May 14, 1942

The flaming crash of the in. bound crack streamline San

Diego, and a truck and railer loaded with it and gasoline at 

Vernon last night had added one today to the toll of traffic dead. and sent six to the hospital for burns and other injuries. 

Reconstructing the spectacular train-truck collision, witnesses said the driver of the truck 

George Ivy, 37, of 402 Penr street, Whittier, who was killed apparently became confused and drove into the path of the stream liner near the intersection of

Downey Road and East Twenty sixth street. 

4 TRAIN CARS BURN 

The train cut the truck from the trailer, tossing the truck off the track and careening for hundreds of yards with the burning trailer wrapped around the nose of the engine, which was badly damaged in the fire. 

Passengers were jolted and thrown to the floor, and the engine crew escaped with burns as the flames raced the length of four cars along the 11-car train. 

Ivy managed to jump from his burning cab, but died an instant later. 

The injured were:

CHRISTI (Tex.) TIMES 

July 27, 1940

An inbound Santa Fe streamliner from San Diego yesterday plowed into a diesel oil truck and trailer in Vernon, ripping off the trailer and showering the right of way with flaming fuel for half a mile. 

Driver of the truck, George Ivy, 37, 238 South Newlin ave., Whittier, was trapped in his cab and was burned to death. 

The fireman of the San Diego flyer, Joseph C. Sutton, 36, 1116 

West 73rd st., was burned about the hands and face, and Leonard 

J. Musso, 37, 3158 Liberty blvd., South Gate, diesel maintenance man for the railway, also suffered burns. 

Earl Hamblin, 44, 2021 Lemon st., Temple City; driver of truck caught in the flames, suffered burns on his left arm. 

Passengers on the train were panicked and shaken up. 

Eli Gerber, San Diego, suffered a back strain. 

Mrs. Wyona W. Beebe, 26, 1010 West 77th st., shock and bruises. 

John McGlamery, United States suffered seaman, navy, suffered shock and bruises. 

Most seriously injured were taken to the Santa Fe hospital. Others were given emergency attention at the scene of the accident. 

Witnesses to the collision were numerous war workers, who had stopped their cars upon seeing the warning signal going at the Downey road crossing, Vernon.

They said the truck driver continued across the tracks just as the crack train reached the intersection. 

Force of the impact split the trailer and bent it like a huge sausage around the front of the engine. 

Burning fuel splattered around the engine and into the yard of the Mouren-Laurens Oil Co., where a fire was started near gasoline storage tanks.

These flames were put out by the Vernon and county fire departments before they reached the tanks. 

The trailer with its load of fuel spilling out from both sides was carried on the nose of the engine for half a mile, giving a fire bath to utility poles and other right of way installations.

The train itself was seared black along the lengths of four cars.

L. R. Burkes, Railway Ex press worker who was in the mail car of the train, said he heard the whistle blasting wildly at 3:15 p. m., and he immediately braced himself in the car in anticipation of a crash. 

A terrific jolt ran through the train, he said, and he hung on to his support until the cars were stopped. 

Then he went to the door, opened it, and flames licked into the car from the river of oil spread along the right of way. 

The death of Ivy brought the county's traffic fatality toll for the year to 388, of which 177 deaths occurred in the city.

Two other victims listed were: 

Vernon C. Glass, 28, 12622 

Foothill ave., Hawthorne. 

Bryan James Horalson, 54, 11952 Cantara st., North Hollywood. 

Glass died in Good Samaritan hospital of injuries suffered last 

Thursday when he was struck by a Pacific Electric train on the 

Figueroa st. crossing near Rosecrans ave. 

Horalson was fatally injured when his car collided at Lankershim blvd. and Strathern ave. with one driven by Claude Looney, 6448 

Tujunga ave.
